I don’t know if I’d say my value as a person, exactly. That sounds… big. I mean, I know being a good husband, showing up to work, doing the job right—those things matter too.

But then again, if I feel like I’m getting out of shape, it does mess with how I see myself. Like I feel lazy or like I’m slipping. My dad used to say you can tell a lot about a guy by how he takes care of his truck, which is funny because half the cars I work on are disasters inside, fast food bags everywhere… but anyway.

I guess I don’t really know where the line is. I don’t think I’m worthless because of my weight, but it definitely affects my head more than I’d like.

I mean… I’m still doing my stuff. I go to work at the garage, I’m not missing shifts or anything, and I still play in my basketball league. Though last week one of the guys was joking about how I run around like I’m still twenty-five, which, you know, I definitely feel it the next morning.

Yeah, I guess I do. Not like I look in the mirror and think I’m huge or anything, but if the scale bumps up even one pound, or my jeans feel tighter, my head goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.”

Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t call it some official diet or anything. It’s more like I’ve got my own little rules, I guess.

Most mornings I just have coffee and get moving. Then later, if things slow down at the shop, I might have a small sandwich, or maybe half of whatever’s around. Nothing like making myself sick or taking pills or whatever.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself preoccupied with your body shape or weight most of the time, even when other people might not notice a change?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, maybe more than I should. It’s not like I’m staring in the mirror all day or anything, but if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, it kind of sticks in my head.\n\nThen I start thinking I’m getting soft, and if I get soft I’ll be slower at work, crawling around cars, or slower on the court. Which sounds ridiculous saying it out loud, because nobody else is probably noticing. My wife probably wouldn’t even—well, I don’t know. But yeah, I notice it, and then I think about it for a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you judge yourself mainly based on your body weight or shape, like feeling that your value as a person depends on it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say my value as a person, exactly. That sounds… big. I mean, I know being a good husband, showing up to work, doing the job right—those things matter too.\n\nBut then again, if I feel like I’m getting out of shape, it does mess with how I see myself. Like I feel lazy or like I’m slipping. My dad used to say you can tell a lot about a guy by how he takes care of his truck, which is funny because half the cars I work on are disasters inside, fast food bags everywhere… but anyway.\n\nI guess I don’t really know where the line is. I don’t think I’m worthless because of my weight, but it definitely affects my head more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your weight or shape ever caused you significant distress or gotten in the way of your daily life, like affecting your relationships, work, or other activities?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still doing my stuff. I go to work at the garage, I’m not missing shifts or anything, and I still play in my basketball league. Though last week one of the guys was joking about how I run around like I’m still twenty-five, which, you know, I definitely feel it the next morning.\n\nBut at home, yeah… it’s caused some friction. I don’t really like sitting down for regular family meals lately, and my wife notices. Then people start asking if I’m okay, or why I’m not eating with everybody, and I get kind of irritated because I don’t want it to be a whole thing. So… it hasn’t stopped my life, exactly, but it’s definitely making home more tense than it should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you’re overweight or out of shape even when other people, or maybe the mirror, don’t really show that you are?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. Not like I look in the mirror and think I’m huge or anything, but if the scale bumps up even one pound, or my jeans feel tighter, my head goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.”\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’ll be slower moving around cars at work, or not as quick up and down the court. Which is probably kind of dumb, because one pound isn’t going to change how I move, but it feels like it means something, you know? Like I’m slipping.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever skip meals, follow a strict diet, or exercise a lot more than usual to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t call it some official diet or anything. It’s more like I’ve got my own little rules, I guess.\n\nMost mornings I just have coffee and get moving. Then later, if things slow down at the shop, I might have a small sandwich, or maybe half of whatever’s around. The shop’s always running late, somebody needs a part, somebody’s car is making a noise they can’t describe—so it’s easy to just keep it simple.\n\nAnd at home it’s busy too, so I kind of feel better sticking to my own routine instead of sitting down to a big meal. It feels more controlled that way, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. My jeans used to fit different when I was younger—well, everybody’s did, I guess—but now I notice every little change.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself sick or use laxatives, diet pills, or anything like that to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Uh… no, I don’t think so. My buddy at work always jokes the younger guys move quicker, so I guess I took that to heart a little.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally trying to lose weight or avoid gaining weight, even if it means eating less than you might want?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I guess I do watch it pretty close. Like if the scale bumps up even a pound, or my jeans feel tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft, you know? And then I’m thinking I’ll be slower getting around under cars at work, or running up and down the court.\n\nIt’s probably stupid, but it gets in my head. I’ll eat less if I think that’s happening. My jeans used to fit different when I was younger—well, everybody’s did, I guess—but now I notice every little change.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself sick or use laxatives, diet pills, or anything like that to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Uh… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’m not doing anything like that. I’ve heard guys at the gym talk about supplements and all that stuff, but I don’t really get into it—half of them are just trying to look good in tank tops, you know? I mostly just try to keep an eye on what I’m eating. Nothing like making myself sick or taking pills or whatever.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself preoccupied with your body shape or weight most of the time, even when other people might not notice a change?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, maybe more than I should. It’s not like I’m staring in the mirror all day or anything, but if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, it kind of sticks in my head.\n\nThen I start thinking I’m getting soft, and if I get soft I’ll be slower at work, crawling around cars, or slower on the court. Which sounds ridiculous saying it out loud, because nobody else is probably noticing. My wife probably wouldn’t even—well, I don’t know. But yeah, I notice it, and then I think about it for a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you judge yourself mainly based on your body weight or shape, like feeling that your value as a person depends on it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say my value as a person, exactly. That sounds… big. I mean, I know being a good husband, showing up to work, doing the job right—those things matter too.\n\nBut then again, if I feel like I’m getting out of shape, it does mess with how I see myself. Like I feel lazy or like I’m slipping. My dad used to say you can tell a lot about a guy by how he takes care of his truck, which is funny because half the cars I work on are disasters inside, fast food bags everywhere… but anyway.\n\nI guess I don’t really know where the line is. I don’t think I’m worthless because of my weight, but it definitely affects my head more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your weight or shape ever caused you significant distress or gotten in the way of your daily life, like affecting your relationships, work, or other activities?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still doing my stuff. I go to work at the garage, I’m not missing shifts or anything, and I still play in my basketball league. Though last week one of the guys was joking about how I run around like I’m still twenty-five, which, you know, I definitely feel it the next morning.\n\nBut at home, yeah… it’s caused some friction. I don’t really like sitting down for regular family meals lately, and my wife notices. Then people start asking if I’m okay, or why I’m not eating with everybody, and I get kind of irritated because I don’t want it to be a whole thing. So… it hasn’t stopped my life, exactly, but it’s definitely making home more tense than it should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you’re overweight or out of shape even when other people, or maybe the mirror, don’t really show that you are?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. Not like I look in the mirror and think I’m huge or anything, but if the scale bumps up even one pound, or my jeans feel tighter, my head goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.”\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’ll be slower moving around cars at work, or not as quick up and down the court. Which is probably kind of dumb, because one pound isn’t going to change how I move, but it feels like it means something, you know? Like I’m slipping.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever skip meals, follow a strict diet, or exercise a lot more than usual to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t call it some official diet or anything. It’s more like I’ve got my own little rules, I guess.\n\nMost mornings I just have coffee and get moving. Then later, if things slow down at the shop, I might have a small sandwich, or maybe half of whatever’s around. The shop’s always running late, somebody needs a part, somebody’s car is making a noise they can’t describe—so it’s easy to just keep it simple.\n\nAnd at home it’s busy too, so I kind of feel better sticking to my own routine instead of sitting down to a big meal. It feels more controlled that way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about your current weight and whether it’s changed recently, and if so, how much weight you may have lost and over what period of time?\n\nPatient: Well, the work physical is kind of what kicked all this off. I had to get checked out for work, and the doctor there said my weight was too low. I don’t know… I guess I didn’t see it that way.\n\nTo me I just feel lighter, like I move better. Playing basketball, I feel quicker up and down the court, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down crawling around or being on my feet all day. So when they made it sound like a problem, I was kind of surprised.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what you currently weigh, and whether that’s changed over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know the exact number off the top of my head… the work physical is what made it a thing, really. The doctor there said I was too low, and I kind of remember thinking, “Low compared to what?” because I’m still doing my job, you know?\n\nLike at the garage, being under cars and on my feet all day, I feel lighter, not dragged down. Same with basketball — I feel quicker getting up and down the court. So yeah, I guess it has gone down some over the past few months, but I didn’t really see it as a problem until that physical.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

  "question": "Can you estimate how much weight you’ve lost over the past few months, even roughly?"

It’s not like I’m staring in the mirror all day or anything, but if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, it kind of sticks in my head.\n\nThen I start thinking I’m getting soft, and if I get soft I’ll be slower at work, crawling around cars, or slower on the court. Which sounds ridiculous saying it out loud, because nobody else is probably noticing. My wife probably wouldn’t even—well, I don’t know. But yeah, I notice it, and then I think about it for a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you judge yourself mainly based on your body weight or shape, like feeling that your value as a person depends on it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say my value as a person, exactly. That sounds… big. I mean, I know being a good husband, showing up to work, doing the job right—those things matter too.\n\nBut then again, if I feel like I’m getting out of shape, it does mess with how I see myself. Like I feel lazy or like I’m slipping. My dad used to say you can tell a lot about a guy by how he takes care of his truck, which is funny because half the cars I work on are disasters inside, fast food bags everywhere… but anyway.\n\nI guess I don’t really know where the line is. I don’t think I’m worthless because of my weight, but it definitely affects my head more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your weight or shape ever caused you significant distress or gotten in the way of your daily life, like affecting your relationships, work, or other activities?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still doing my stuff. I go to work at the garage, I’m not missing shifts or anything, and I still play in my basketball league. Though last week one of the guys was joking about how I run around like I’m still twenty-five, which, you know, I definitely feel it the next morning.\n\nBut at home, yeah… it’s caused some friction. I don’t really like sitting down for regular family meals lately, and my wife notices. Then people start asking if I’m okay, or why I’m not eating with everybody, and I get kind of irritated because I don’t want it to be a whole thing. So… it hasn’t stopped my life, exactly, but it’s definitely making home more tense than it should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you’re overweight or out of shape even when other people, or maybe the mirror, don’t really show that you are?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. Not like I look in the mirror and think I’m huge or anything, but if the scale bumps up even one pound, or my jeans feel tighter, my head goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.”\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’ll be slower moving around cars at work, or not as quick up and down the court. Which is probably kind of dumb, because one pound isn’t going to change how I move, but it feels like it means something, you know? Like I’m slipping.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever skip meals, follow a strict diet, or exercise a lot more than usual to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t call it some official diet or anything. It’s more like I’ve got my own little rules, I guess.\n\nMost mornings I just have coffee and get moving. Then later, if things slow down at the shop, I might have a small sandwich, or maybe half of whatever’s around. The shop’s always running late, somebody needs a part, somebody’s car is making a noise they can’t describe—so it’s easy to just keep it simple.\n\nAnd at home it’s busy too, so I kind of feel better sticking to my own routine instead of sitting down to a big meal. It feels more controlled that way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about your current weight and whether it’s changed recently, and if so, how much weight you may have lost and over what period of time?\n\nPatient: Well, the work physical is kind of what kicked all this off. I had to get checked out for work, and the doctor there said my weight was too low. I don’t know… I guess I didn’t see it that way.\n\nTo me I just feel lighter, like I move better. Playing basketball, I feel quicker up and down the court, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down crawling around or being on my feet all day. So when they made it sound like a problem, I was kind of surprised.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what you currently weigh, and whether that’s changed over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know the exact number off the top of my head… the work physical is what made it a thing, really. The doctor there said I was too low, and I kind of remember thinking, “Low compared to what?” because I’m still doing my job, you know?\n\nLike at the garage, being under cars and on my feet all day, I feel lighter, not dragged down. Same with basketball — I feel quicker getting up and down the court. So yeah, I guess it has gone down some over the past few months, but I didn’t really see it as a problem until that physical.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much weight you’ve lost over the past few months, even roughly?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t have a clean number for you. That’s part of why this feels strange, because I wasn’t really tracking it like that.\n\nThe work physical is what got me sitting here, honestly. The doctor there said my weight was too low, and I guess I heard it, but in my head I’m thinking, well, I’m still moving around fine. At basketball I feel lighter on my feet, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down when I’m up and down all day.\n\nSo… roughly, yeah, it’s gone down, but I don’t know how much to say exactly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

Nothing like making myself sick or taking pills or whatever.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself preoccupied with your body shape or weight most of the time, even when other people might not notice a change?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, maybe more than I should. It’s not like I’m staring in the mirror all day or anything, but if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, it kind of sticks in my head.\n\nThen I start thinking I’m getting soft, and if I get soft I’ll be slower at work, crawling around cars, or slower on the court. Which sounds ridiculous saying it out loud, because nobody else is probably noticing. My wife probably wouldn’t even—well, I don’t know. But yeah, I notice it, and then I think about it for a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you judge yourself mainly based on your body weight or shape, like feeling that your value as a person depends on it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say my value as a person, exactly. That sounds… big. I mean, I know being a good husband, showing up to work, doing the job right—those things matter too.\n\nBut then again, if I feel like I’m getting out of shape, it does mess with how I see myself. Like I feel lazy or like I’m slipping. My dad used to say you can tell a lot about a guy by how he takes care of his truck, which is funny because half the cars I work on are disasters inside, fast food bags everywhere… but anyway.\n\nI guess I don’t really know where the line is. I don’t think I’m worthless because of my weight, but it definitely affects my head more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your weight or shape ever caused you significant distress or gotten in the way of your daily life, like affecting your relationships, work, or other activities?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still doing my stuff. I go to work at the garage, I’m not missing shifts or anything, and I still play in my basketball league. Though last week one of the guys was joking about how I run around like I’m still twenty-five, which, you know, I definitely feel it the next morning.\n\nBut at home, yeah… it’s caused some friction. I don’t really like sitting down for regular family meals lately, and my wife notices. Then people start asking if I’m okay, or why I’m not eating with everybody, and I get kind of irritated because I don’t want it to be a whole thing. So… it hasn’t stopped my life, exactly, but it’s definitely making home more tense than it should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you’re overweight or out of shape even when other people, or maybe the mirror, don’t really show that you are?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. Not like I look in the mirror and think I’m huge or anything, but if the scale bumps up even one pound, or my jeans feel tighter, my head goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.”\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’ll be slower moving around cars at work, or not as quick up and down the court. Which is probably kind of dumb, because one pound isn’t going to change how I move, but it feels like it means something, you know? Like I’m slipping.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever skip meals, follow a strict diet, or exercise a lot more than usual to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t call it some official diet or anything. It’s more like I’ve got my own little rules, I guess.\n\nMost mornings I just have coffee and get moving. Then later, if things slow down at the shop, I might have a small sandwich, or maybe half of whatever’s around. The shop’s always running late, somebody needs a part, somebody’s car is making a noise they can’t describe—so it’s easy to just keep it simple.\n\nAnd at home it’s busy too, so I kind of feel better sticking to my own routine instead of sitting down to a big meal. It feels more controlled that way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about your current weight and whether it’s changed recently, and if so, how much weight you may have lost and over what period of time?\n\nPatient: Well, the work physical is kind of what kicked all this off. I had to get checked out for work, and the doctor there said my weight was too low. I don’t know… I guess I didn’t see it that way.\n\nTo me I just feel lighter, like I move better. Playing basketball, I feel quicker up and down the court, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down crawling around or being on my feet all day. So when they made it sound like a problem, I was kind of surprised.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what you currently weigh, and whether that’s changed over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know the exact number off the top of my head… the work physical is what made it a thing, really. The doctor there said I was too low, and I kind of remember thinking, “Low compared to what?” because I’m still doing my job, you know?\n\nLike at the garage, being under cars and on my feet all day, I feel lighter, not dragged down. Same with basketball — I feel quicker getting up and down the court. So yeah, I guess it has gone down some over the past few months, but I didn’t really see it as a problem until that physical.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much weight you’ve lost over the past few months, even roughly?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t have a clean number for you. That’s part of why this feels strange, because I wasn’t really tracking it like that.\n\nThe work physical is what got me sitting here, honestly. The doctor there said my weight was too low, and I guess I heard it, but in my head I’m thinking, well, I’m still moving around fine. At basketball I feel lighter on my feet, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down when I’m up and down all day.\n\nSo… roughly, yeah, it’s gone down, but I don’t know how much to say exactly.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the weight loss was something you were intentionally trying to achieve, even if it meant staying below what might be a normal weight for your height and build?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I was trying to be, like, under some number. I don’t really think of it that way.\n\nI guess I do have my own way of doing things now, though. Most mornings it’s just coffee, then later maybe a small sandwich or half of whatever’s around. The shop runs late a lot, and then the house is busy when I get home, and honestly it just feels easier sticking to that. Like if I keep it simple, I feel better, less… I don’t know, thrown off.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’m choosing it in that sense, but I wasn’t sitting there thinking about a “normal weight” chart or anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1, F50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, uh, mostly because of my work physical. The doctor there said my weight was too low and that I should come in, so… here I am. I don’t know, I don’t really feel bad about it. Honestly I feel kind of lighter on my feet when I play basketball, and at the garage it’s nice not feeling so weighed down crawling around cars all day. My buddy at work always jokes the younger guys move quicker, so I guess I took that to heart a little.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally trying to lose weight or avoid gaining weight, even if it means eating less than you might want?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I guess I do watch it pretty close. Like if the scale bumps up even a pound, or my jeans feel tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft, you know? And then I’m thinking I’ll be slower getting around under cars at work, or running up and down the court.\n\nIt’s probably stupid, but it gets in my head. I’ll eat less if I think that’s happening. My jeans used to fit different when I was younger—well, everybody’s did, I guess—but now I notice every little change.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself sick or use laxatives, diet pills, or anything like that to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Uh… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’m not doing anything like that. I’ve heard guys at the gym talk about supplements and all that stuff, but I don’t really get into it—half of them are just trying to look good in tank tops, you know? I mostly just try to keep an eye on what I’m eating. Nothing like making myself sick or taking pills or whatever.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself preoccupied with your body shape or weight most of the time, even when other people might not notice a change?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, maybe more than I should. It’s not like I’m staring in the mirror all day or anything, but if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, it kind of sticks in my head.\n\nThen I start thinking I’m getting soft, and if I get soft I’ll be slower at work, crawling around cars, or slower on the court. Which sounds ridiculous saying it out loud, because nobody else is probably noticing. My wife probably wouldn’t even—well, I don’t know. But yeah, I notice it, and then I think about it for a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you judge yourself mainly based on your body weight or shape, like feeling that your value as a person depends on it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say my value as a person, exactly. That sounds… big. I mean, I know being a good husband, showing up to work, doing the job right—those things matter too.\n\nBut then again, if I feel like I’m getting out of shape, it does mess with how I see myself. Like I feel lazy or like I’m slipping. My dad used to say you can tell a lot about a guy by how he takes care of his truck, which is funny because half the cars I work on are disasters inside, fast food bags everywhere… but anyway.\n\nI guess I don’t really know where the line is. I don’t think I’m worthless because of my weight, but it definitely affects my head more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your weight or shape ever caused you significant distress or gotten in the way of your daily life, like affecting your relationships, work, or other activities?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still doing my stuff. I go to work at the garage, I’m not missing shifts or anything, and I still play in my basketball league. Though last week one of the guys was joking about how I run around like I’m still twenty-five, which, you know, I definitely feel it the next morning.\n\nBut at home, yeah… it’s caused some friction. I don’t really like sitting down for regular family meals lately, and my wife notices. Then people start asking if I’m okay, or why I’m not eating with everybody, and I get kind of irritated because I don’t want it to be a whole thing. So… it hasn’t stopped my life, exactly, but it’s definitely making home more tense than it should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you’re overweight or out of shape even when other people, or maybe the mirror, don’t really show that you are?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. Not like I look in the mirror and think I’m huge or anything, but if the scale bumps up even one pound, or my jeans feel tighter, my head goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.”\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’ll be slower moving around cars at work, or not as quick up and down the court. Which is probably kind of dumb, because one pound isn’t going to change how I move, but it feels like it means something, you know? Like I’m slipping.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever skip meals, follow a strict diet, or exercise a lot more than usual to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t call it some official diet or anything. It’s more like I’ve got my own little rules, I guess.\n\nMost mornings I just have coffee and get moving. Then later, if things slow down at the shop, I might have a small sandwich, or maybe half of whatever’s around. The shop’s always running late, somebody needs a part, somebody’s car is making a noise they can’t describe—so it’s easy to just keep it simple.\n\nAnd at home it’s busy too, so I kind of feel better sticking to my own routine instead of sitting down to a big meal. It feels more controlled that way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about your current weight and whether it’s changed recently, and if so, how much weight you may have lost and over what period of time?\n\nPatient: Well, the work physical is kind of what kicked all this off. I had to get checked out for work, and the doctor there said my weight was too low. I don’t know… I guess I didn’t see it that way.\n\nTo me I just feel lighter, like I move better. Playing basketball, I feel quicker up and down the court, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down crawling around or being on my feet all day. So when they made it sound like a problem, I was kind of surprised.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what you currently weigh, and whether that’s changed over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know the exact number off the top of my head… the work physical is what made it a thing, really. The doctor there said I was too low, and I kind of remember thinking, “Low compared to what?” because I’m still doing my job, you know?\n\nLike at the garage, being under cars and on my feet all day, I feel lighter, not dragged down. Same with basketball — I feel quicker getting up and down the court. So yeah, I guess it has gone down some over the past few months, but I didn’t really see it as a problem until that physical.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much weight you’ve lost over the past few months, even roughly?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t have a clean number for you. That’s part of why this feels strange, because I wasn’t really tracking it like that.\n\nThe work physical is what got me sitting here, honestly. The doctor there said my weight was too low, and I guess I heard it, but in my head I’m thinking, well, I’m still moving around fine. At basketball I feel lighter on my feet, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down when I’m up and down all day.\n\nSo… roughly, yeah, it’s gone down, but I don’t know how much to say exactly.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the weight loss was something you were intentionally trying to achieve, even if it meant staying below what might be a normal weight for your height and build?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I was trying to be, like, under some number. I don’t really think of it that way.\n\nI guess I do have my own way of doing things now, though. Most mornings it’s just coffee, then later maybe a small sandwich or half of whatever’s around. The shop runs late a lot, and then the house is busy when I get home, and honestly it just feels easier sticking to that. Like if I keep it simple, I feel better, less… I don’t know, thrown off.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’m choosing it in that sense, but I wasn’t sitting there thinking about a “normal weight” chart or anything.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me approximately what your current weight is, and how that compares to what you think is a normal or healthy weight for your height and build?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure of the exact number right now. The work physical is what kind of kicked all this off — the doctor there said my weight was too low for me, I guess for my height and build.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to see it that way, because at basketball I just feel lighter on my feet, like I can move better. And at the garage, you know, I’m crawling around, standing, bending all day, and I don’t feel as weighed down. So compared to what they’re calling normal or healthy, yeah, I guess I’m under that… I just don’t always feel like it’s a bad thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. My dad used to say you can tell a lot about a guy by how he takes care of his truck, which is funny because half the cars I work on are disasters inside, fast food bags everywhere… but anyway.\n\nI guess I don’t really know where the line is. I don’t think I’m worthless because of my weight, but it definitely affects my head more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your weight or shape ever caused you significant distress or gotten in the way of your daily life, like affecting your relationships, work, or other activities?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still doing my stuff. I go to work at the garage, I’m not missing shifts or anything, and I still play in my basketball league. Though last week one of the guys was joking about how I run around like I’m still twenty-five, which, you know, I definitely feel it the next morning.\n\nBut at home, yeah… it’s caused some friction. I don’t really like sitting down for regular family meals lately, and my wife notices. Then people start asking if I’m okay, or why I’m not eating with everybody, and I get kind of irritated because I don’t want it to be a whole thing. So… it hasn’t stopped my life, exactly, but it’s definitely making home more tense than it should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you’re overweight or out of shape even when other people, or maybe the mirror, don’t really show that you are?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. Not like I look in the mirror and think I’m huge or anything, but if the scale bumps up even one pound, or my jeans feel tighter, my head goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.”\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’ll be slower moving around cars at work, or not as quick up and down the court. Which is probably kind of dumb, because one pound isn’t going to change how I move, but it feels like it means something, you know? Like I’m slipping.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever skip meals, follow a strict diet, or exercise a lot more than usual to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t call it some official diet or anything. It’s more like I’ve got my own little rules, I guess.\n\nMost mornings I just have coffee and get moving. Then later, if things slow down at the shop, I might have a small sandwich, or maybe half of whatever’s around. The shop’s always running late, somebody needs a part, somebody’s car is making a noise they can’t describe—so it’s easy to just keep it simple.\n\nAnd at home it’s busy too, so I kind of feel better sticking to my own routine instead of sitting down to a big meal. It feels more controlled that way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about your current weight and whether it’s changed recently, and if so, how much weight you may have lost and over what period of time?\n\nPatient: Well, the work physical is kind of what kicked all this off. I had to get checked out for work, and the doctor there said my weight was too low. I don’t know… I guess I didn’t see it that way.\n\nTo me I just feel lighter, like I move better. Playing basketball, I feel quicker up and down the court, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down crawling around or being on my feet all day. So when they made it sound like a problem, I was kind of surprised.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what you currently weigh, and whether that’s changed over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know the exact number off the top of my head… the work physical is what made it a thing, really. The doctor there said I was too low, and I kind of remember thinking, “Low compared to what?” because I’m still doing my job, you know?\n\nLike at the garage, being under cars and on my feet all day, I feel lighter, not dragged down. Same with basketball — I feel quicker getting up and down the court. So yeah, I guess it has gone down some over the past few months, but I didn’t really see it as a problem until that physical.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much weight you’ve lost over the past few months, even roughly?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t have a clean number for you. That’s part of why this feels strange, because I wasn’t really tracking it like that.\n\nThe work physical is what got me sitting here, honestly. The doctor there said my weight was too low, and I guess I heard it, but in my head I’m thinking, well, I’m still moving around fine. At basketball I feel lighter on my feet, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down when I’m up and down all day.\n\nSo… roughly, yeah, it’s gone down, but I don’t know how much to say exactly.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the weight loss was something you were intentionally trying to achieve, even if it meant staying below what might be a normal weight for your height and build?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I was trying to be, like, under some number. I don’t really think of it that way.\n\nI guess I do have my own way of doing things now, though. Most mornings it’s just coffee, then later maybe a small sandwich or half of whatever’s around. The shop runs late a lot, and then the house is busy when I get home, and honestly it just feels easier sticking to that. Like if I keep it simple, I feel better, less… I don’t know, thrown off.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’m choosing it in that sense, but I wasn’t sitting there thinking about a “normal weight” chart or anything.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me approximately what your current weight is, and how that compares to what you think is a normal or healthy weight for your height and build?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure of the exact number right now. The work physical is what kind of kicked all this off — the doctor there said my weight was too low for me, I guess for my height and build.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to see it that way, because at basketball I just feel lighter on my feet, like I can move better. And at the garage, you know, I’m crawling around, standing, bending all day, and I don’t feel as weighed down. So compared to what they’re calling normal or healthy, yeah, I guess I’m under that… I just don’t always feel like it’s a bad thing.\n\nDoctor: Even though you said you weren’t focused on a specific number, do you think you’re currently under what most people would consider a normal or healthy weight for someone your height?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess if you put it that way, probably. The doctor at the work physical said my weight was too low, and that’s really what got me here.\n\nIt’s just hard for me to think of it like “unhealthy,” because when I’m playing basketball I feel quicker, lighter on my feet. And at the garage, being up and down all day, leaning over cars and moving around, I don’t feel as weighed down. So… yeah, most people might say I’m under where I should be, but I don’t really feel it the same way.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what your height is and whether the doctor at your work physical mentioned how your current weight compares to the minimum normal weight for someone your height?\n\nPatient: I’m not great with the exact numbers off the top of my head. I mean, I know my height, but I don’t really keep track of it like that, and the work physical is what sort of got this whole thing started.\n\nThe doctor there said my weight was too low for me, like for my height, yeah. I don’t remember him saying it like “minimum normal weight” exactly, or maybe he did and I just kind of tuned out after “too low.” I was thinking about getting back to the garage because we were slammed that week.\n\nBut yeah, he was basically saying I was under where I should be. I just… I don’t feel it the same way, you know? Playing basketball, I feel lighter on my feet, and at work I don’t feel as weighed down moving around cars all day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Playing basketball, I feel lighter on my feet, and at work I don’t feel as weighed down moving around cars all day.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you tell me your exact height and what your current weight is, so I can better understand how it compares?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 44 / automotive_service_technician_or_mechanic / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Low_Weight\nmanifestation: \"My work physical is what got me here—the doctor said my weight is too low, but to me I just feel lighter on my feet for basketball and not as weighed down at the garage.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know the exact weight right now… I know that’s what you’re asking, but I haven’t really been keeping a number in my head.

The work physical is what got me here, really. That doctor said my weight was too low for me, like for my height. I kind of heard that and then my mind went to work stuff — we had a bunch of cars backed up at the garage, and I was thinking about getting back there.

But yeah, he said it was low. I just have a hard time feeling worried about it, because on the basketball court I feel lighter, like I can move better, and at work I don’t feel as weighed down crawling around and bending over cars all day.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, uh, mostly because of my work physical. The doctor there said my weight was too low and that I should come in, so… here I am. I don’t know, I don’t really feel bad about it. Honestly I feel kind of lighter on my feet when I play basketball, and at the garage it’s nice not feeling so weighed down crawling around cars all day. My buddy at work always jokes the younger guys move quicker, so I guess I took that to heart a little.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally trying to lose weight or avoid gaining weight, even if it means eating less than you might want?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I guess I do watch it pretty close. Like if the scale bumps up even a pound, or my jeans feel tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft, you know? And then I’m thinking I’ll be slower getting around under cars at work, or running up and down the court.\n\nIt’s probably stupid, but it gets in my head. I’ll eat less if I think that’s happening. My jeans used to fit different when I was younger—well, everybody’s did, I guess—but now I notice every little change.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself sick or use laxatives, diet pills, or anything like that to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Uh… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’m not doing anything like that. I’ve heard guys at the gym talk about supplements and all that stuff, but I don’t really get into it—half of them are just trying to look good in tank tops, you know? I mostly just try to keep an eye on what I’m eating. Nothing like making myself sick or taking pills or whatever.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself preoccupied with your body shape or weight most of the time, even when other people might not notice a change?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, maybe more than I should. It’s not like I’m staring in the mirror all day or anything, but if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, it kind of sticks in my head.\n\nThen I start thinking I’m getting soft, and if I get soft I’ll be slower at work, crawling around cars, or slower on the court. Which sounds ridiculous saying it out loud, because nobody else is probably noticing. My wife probably wouldn’t even—well, I don’t know. But yeah, I notice it, and then I think about it for a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you judge yourself mainly based on your body weight or shape, like feeling that your value as a person depends on it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say my value as a person, exactly. That sounds… big. I mean, I know being a good husband, showing up to work, doing the job right—those things matter too.\n\nBut then again, if I feel like I’m getting out of shape, it does mess with how I see myself. Like I feel lazy or like I’m slipping. My dad used to say you can tell a lot about a guy by how he takes care of his truck, which is funny because half the cars I work on are disasters inside, fast food bags everywhere… but anyway.\n\nI guess I don’t really know where the line is. I don’t think I’m worthless because of my weight, but it definitely affects my head more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your weight or shape ever caused you significant distress or gotten in the way of your daily life, like affecting your relationships, work, or other activities?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still doing my stuff. I go to work at the garage, I’m not missing shifts or anything, and I still play in my basketball league. Though last week one of the guys was joking about how I run around like I’m still twenty-five, which, you know, I definitely feel it the next morning.\n\nBut at home, yeah… it’s caused some friction. I don’t really like sitting down for regular family meals lately, and my wife notices. Then people start asking if I’m okay, or why I’m not eating with everybody, and I get kind of irritated because I don’t want it to be a whole thing. So… it hasn’t stopped my life, exactly, but it’s definitely making home more tense than it should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you’re overweight or out of shape even when other people, or maybe the mirror, don’t really show that you are?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. Not like I look in the mirror and think I’m huge or anything, but if the scale bumps up even one pound, or my jeans feel tighter, my head goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.”\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’ll be slower moving around cars at work, or not as quick up and down the court. Which is probably kind of dumb, because one pound isn’t going to change how I move, but it feels like it means something, you know? Like I’m slipping.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever skip meals, follow a strict diet, or exercise a lot more than usual to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t call it some official diet or anything. It’s more like I’ve got my own little rules, I guess.\n\nMost mornings I just have coffee and get moving. Then later, if things slow down at the shop, I might have a small sandwich, or maybe half of whatever’s around. The shop’s always running late, somebody needs a part, somebody’s car is making a noise they can’t describe—so it’s easy to just keep it simple.\n\nAnd at home it’s busy too, so I kind of feel better sticking to my own routine instead of sitting down to a big meal. It feels more controlled that way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about your current weight and whether it’s changed recently, and if so, how much weight you may have lost and over what period of time?\n\nPatient: Well, the work physical is kind of what kicked all this off. I had to get checked out for work, and the doctor there said my weight was too low. I don’t know… I guess I didn’t see it that way.\n\nTo me I just feel lighter, like I move better. Playing basketball, I feel quicker up and down the court, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down crawling around or being on my feet all day. So when they made it sound like a problem, I was kind of surprised.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what you currently weigh, and whether that’s changed over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know the exact number off the top of my head… the work physical is what made it a thing, really. The doctor there said I was too low, and I kind of remember thinking, “Low compared to what?” because I’m still doing my job, you know?\n\nLike at the garage, being under cars and on my feet all day, I feel lighter, not dragged down. Same with basketball — I feel quicker getting up and down the court. So yeah, I guess it has gone down some over the past few months, but I didn’t really see it as a problem until that physical.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much weight you’ve lost over the past few months, even roughly?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t have a clean number for you. That’s part of why this feels strange, because I wasn’t really tracking it like that.\n\nThe work physical is what got me sitting here, honestly. The doctor there said my weight was too low, and I guess I heard it, but in my head I’m thinking, well, I’m still moving around fine. At basketball I feel lighter on my feet, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down when I’m up and down all day.\n\nSo… roughly, yeah, it’s gone down, but I don’t know how much to say exactly.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the weight loss was something you were intentionally trying to achieve, even if it meant staying below what might be a normal weight for your height and build?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I was trying to be, like, under some number. I don’t really think of it that way.\n\nI guess I do have my own way of doing things now, though. Most mornings it’s just coffee, then later maybe a small sandwich or half of whatever’s around. The shop runs late a lot, and then the house is busy when I get home, and honestly it just feels easier sticking to that. Like if I keep it simple, I feel better, less… I don’t know, thrown off.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’m choosing it in that sense, but I wasn’t sitting there thinking about a “normal weight” chart or anything.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me approximately what your current weight is, and how that compares to what you think is a normal or healthy weight for your height and build?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure of the exact number right now. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1, F50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, uh, mostly because of my work physical. The doctor there said my weight was too low and that I should come in, so… here I am. I don’t know, I don’t really feel bad about it. Honestly I feel kind of lighter on my feet when I play basketball, and at the garage it’s nice not feeling so weighed down crawling around cars all day. My buddy at work always jokes the younger guys move quicker, so I guess I took that to heart a little.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally trying to lose weight or avoid gaining weight, even if it means eating less than you might want?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I guess I do watch it pretty close. Like if the scale bumps up even a pound, or my jeans feel tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft, you know? And then I’m thinking I’ll be slower getting around under cars at work, or running up and down the court.\n\nIt’s probably stupid, but it gets in my head. I’ll eat less if I think that’s happening. My jeans used to fit different when I was younger—well, everybody’s did, I guess—but now I notice every little change.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself sick or use laxatives, diet pills, or anything like that to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Uh… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’m not doing anything like that. I’ve heard guys at the gym talk about supplements and all that stuff, but I don’t really get into it—half of them are just trying to look good in tank tops, you know? I mostly just try to keep an eye on what I’m eating. Nothing like making myself sick or taking pills or whatever.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself preoccupied with your body shape or weight most of the time, even when other people might not notice a change?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, maybe more than I should. It’s not like I’m staring in the mirror all day or anything, but if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, it kind of sticks in my head.\n\nThen I start thinking I’m getting soft, and if I get soft I’ll be slower at work, crawling around cars, or slower on the court. Which sounds ridiculous saying it out loud, because nobody else is probably noticing. My wife probably wouldn’t even—well, I don’t know. But yeah, I notice it, and then I think about it for a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you judge yourself mainly based on your body weight or shape, like feeling that your value as a person depends on it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say my value as a person, exactly. That sounds… big. I mean, I know being a good husband, showing up to work, doing the job right—those things matter too.\n\nBut then again, if I feel like I’m getting out of shape, it does mess with how I see myself. Like I feel lazy or like I’m slipping. My dad used to say you can tell a lot about a guy by how he takes care of his truck, which is funny because half the cars I work on are disasters inside, fast food bags everywhere… but anyway.\n\nI guess I don’t really know where the line is. I don’t think I’m worthless because of my weight, but it definitely affects my head more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your weight or shape ever caused you significant distress or gotten in the way of your daily life, like affecting your relationships, work, or other activities?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still doing my stuff. I go to work at the garage, I’m not missing shifts or anything, and I still play in my basketball league. Though last week one of the guys was joking about how I run around like I’m still twenty-five, which, you know, I definitely feel it the next morning.\n\nBut at home, yeah… it’s caused some friction. I don’t really like sitting down for regular family meals lately, and my wife notices. Then people start asking if I’m okay, or why I’m not eating with everybody, and I get kind of irritated because I don’t want it to be a whole thing. So… it hasn’t stopped my life, exactly, but it’s definitely making home more tense than it should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you’re overweight or out of shape even when other people, or maybe the mirror, don’t really show that you are?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. Not like I look in the mirror and think I’m huge or anything, but if the scale bumps up even one pound, or my jeans feel tighter, my head goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.”\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’ll be slower moving around cars at work, or not as quick up and down the court. Which is probably kind of dumb, because one pound isn’t going to change how I move, but it feels like it means something, you know? Like I’m slipping.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever skip meals, follow a strict diet, or exercise a lot more than usual to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t call it some official diet or anything. It’s more like I’ve got my own little rules, I guess.\n\nMost mornings I just have coffee and get moving. Then later, if things slow down at the shop, I might have a small sandwich, or maybe half of whatever’s around. The shop’s always running late, somebody needs a part, somebody’s car is making a noise they can’t describe—so it’s easy to just keep it simple.\n\nAnd at home it’s busy too, so I kind of feel better sticking to my own routine instead of sitting down to a big meal. It feels more controlled that way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about your current weight and whether it’s changed recently, and if so, how much weight you may have lost and over what period of time?\n\nPatient: Well, the work physical is kind of what kicked all this off. I had to get checked out for work, and the doctor there said my weight was too low. I don’t know… I guess I didn’t see it that way.\n\nTo me I just feel lighter, like I move better. Playing basketball, I feel quicker up and down the court, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down crawling around or being on my feet all day. So when they made it sound like a problem, I was kind of surprised.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what you currently weigh, and whether that’s changed over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know the exact number off the top of my head… the work physical is what made it a thing, really. The doctor there said I was too low, and I kind of remember thinking, “Low compared to what?” because I’m still doing my job, you know?\n\nLike at the garage, being under cars and on my feet all day, I feel lighter, not dragged down. Same with basketball — I feel quicker getting up and down the court. So yeah, I guess it has gone down some over the past few months, but I didn’t really see it as a problem until that physical.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much weight you’ve lost over the past few months, even roughly?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t have a clean number for you. That’s part of why this feels strange, because I wasn’t really tracking it like that.\n\nThe work physical is what got me sitting here, honestly. The doctor there said my weight was too low, and I guess I heard it, but in my head I’m thinking, well, I’m still moving around fine. At basketball I feel lighter on my feet, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down when I’m up and down all day.\n\nSo… roughly, yeah, it’s gone down, but I don’t know how much to say exactly.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the weight loss was something you were intentionally trying to achieve, even if it meant staying below what might be a normal weight for your height and build?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I was trying to be, like, under some number. I don’t really think of it that way.\n\nI guess I do have my own way of doing things now, though. Most mornings it’s just coffee, then later maybe a small sandwich or half of whatever’s around. The shop runs late a lot, and then the house is busy when I get home, and honestly it just feels easier sticking to that. Like if I keep it simple, I feel better, less… I don’t know, thrown off.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’m choosing it in that sense, but I wasn’t sitting there thinking about a “normal weight” chart or anything.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me approximately what your current weight is, and how that compares to what you think is a normal or healthy weight for your height and build?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure of the exact number right now. The work physical is what kind of kicked all this off — the doctor there said my weight was too low for me, I guess for my height and build.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to see it that way, because at basketball I just feel lighter on my feet, like I can move better. And at the garage, you know, I’m crawling around, standing, bending all day, and I don’t feel as weighed down. So compared to what they’re calling normal or healthy, yeah, I guess I’m under that… I just don’t always feel like it’s a bad thing.\n\nDoctor: Even though you said you weren’t focused on a specific number, do you think you’re currently under what most people would consider a normal or healthy weight for someone your height?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess if you put it that way, probably. The doctor at the work physical said my weight was too low, and that’s really what got me here.\n\nIt’s just hard for me to think of it like “unhealthy,” because when I’m playing basketball I feel quicker, lighter on my feet. So… yeah, most people might say I’m under where I should be, but I don’t really feel it the same way.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what your height is and whether the doctor at your work physical mentioned how your current weight compares to the minimum normal weight for someone your height?\n\nPatient: I’m not great with the exact numbers off the top of my head. The shop’s always running late, somebody needs a part, somebody’s car is making a noise they can’t describe—so it’s easy to just keep it simple.\n\nAnd at home it’s busy too, so I kind of feel better sticking to my own routine instead of sitting down to a big meal. It feels more controlled that way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about your current weight and whether it’s changed recently, and if so, how much weight you may have lost and over what period of time?\n\nPatient: Well, the work physical is kind of what kicked all this off. I had to get checked out for work, and the doctor there said my weight was too low. I don’t know… I guess I didn’t see it that way.\n\nTo me I just feel lighter, like I move better. Playing basketball, I feel quicker up and down the court, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down crawling around or being on my feet all day. So when they made it sound like a problem, I was kind of surprised.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what you currently weigh, and whether that’s changed over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know the exact number off the top of my head… the work physical is what made it a thing, really. The doctor there said I was too low, and I kind of remember thinking, “Low compared to what?” because I’m still doing my job, you know?\n\nLike at the garage, being under cars and on my feet all day, I feel lighter, not dragged down. Same with basketball — I feel quicker getting up and down the court. So yeah, I guess it has gone down some over the past few months, but I didn’t really see it as a problem until that physical.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much weight you’ve lost over the past few months, even roughly?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t have a clean number for you. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, uh, mostly because of my work physical. The doctor there said my weight was too low and that I should come in, so… here I am. I don’t know, I don’t really feel bad about it. Honestly I feel kind of lighter on my feet when I play basketball, and at the garage it’s nice not feeling so weighed down crawling around cars all day. My buddy at work always jokes the younger guys move quicker, so I guess I took that to heart a little.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally trying to lose weight or avoid gaining weight, even if it means eating less than you might want?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I guess I do watch it pretty close. Like if the scale bumps up even a pound, or my jeans feel tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft, you know? And then I’m thinking I’ll be slower getting around under cars at work, or running up and down the court.\n\nIt’s probably stupid, but it gets in my head. I’ll eat less if I think that’s happening. My jeans used to fit different when I was younger—well, everybody’s did, I guess—but now I notice every little change.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself sick or use laxatives, diet pills, or anything like that to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Uh… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’m not doing anything like that. I’ve heard guys at the gym talk about supplements and all that stuff, but I don’t really get into it—half of them are just trying to look good in tank tops, you know? I mostly just try to keep an eye on what I’m eating. Nothing like making myself sick or taking pills or whatever.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself preoccupied with your body shape or weight most of the time, even when other people might not notice a change?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, maybe more than I should. It’s not like I’m staring in the mirror all day or anything, but if I see the scale go up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, it kind of sticks in my head.\n\nThen I start thinking I’m getting soft, and if I get soft I’ll be slower at work, crawling around cars, or slower on the court. Which sounds ridiculous saying it out loud, because nobody else is probably noticing. My wife probably wouldn’t even—well, I don’t know. But yeah, I notice it, and then I think about it for a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you judge yourself mainly based on your body weight or shape, like feeling that your value as a person depends on it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say my value as a person, exactly. That sounds… big. I mean, I know being a good husband, showing up to work, doing the job right—those things matter too.\n\nBut then again, if I feel like I’m getting out of shape, it does mess with how I see myself. Like I feel lazy or like I’m slipping. My dad used to say you can tell a lot about a guy by how he takes care of his truck, which is funny because half the cars I work on are disasters inside, fast food bags everywhere… but anyway.\n\nI guess I don’t really know where the line is. I don’t think I’m worthless because of my weight, but it definitely affects my head more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your weight or shape ever caused you significant distress or gotten in the way of your daily life, like affecting your relationships, work, or other activities?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still doing my stuff. I go to work at the garage, I’m not missing shifts or anything, and I still play in my basketball league. Though last week one of the guys was joking about how I run around like I’m still twenty-five, which, you know, I definitely feel it the next morning.\n\nBut at home, yeah… it’s caused some friction. I don’t really like sitting down for regular family meals lately, and my wife notices. Then people start asking if I’m okay, or why I’m not eating with everybody, and I get kind of irritated because I don’t want it to be a whole thing. So… it hasn’t stopped my life, exactly, but it’s definitely making home more tense than it should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you’re overweight or out of shape even when other people, or maybe the mirror, don’t really show that you are?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. Not like I look in the mirror and think I’m huge or anything, but if the scale bumps up even one pound, or my jeans feel tighter, my head goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.”\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’ll be slower moving around cars at work, or not as quick up and down the court. Which is probably kind of dumb, because one pound isn’t going to change how I move, but it feels like it means something, you know? Like I’m slipping.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever skip meals, follow a strict diet, or exercise a lot more than usual to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t call it some official diet or anything. It’s more like I’ve got my own little rules, I guess.\n\nMost mornings I just have coffee and get moving. Then later, if things slow down at the shop, I might have a small sandwich, or maybe half of whatever’s around. The shop’s always running late, somebody needs a part, somebody’s car is making a noise they can’t describe—so it’s easy to just keep it simple.\n\nAnd at home it’s busy too, so I kind of feel better sticking to my own routine instead of sitting down to a big meal. It feels more controlled that way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about your current weight and whether it’s changed recently, and if so, how much weight you may have lost and over what period of time?\n\nPatient: Well, the work physical is kind of what kicked all this off. I had to get checked out for work, and the doctor there said my weight was too low. I don’t know… I guess I didn’t see it that way.\n\nTo me I just feel lighter, like I move better. Playing basketball, I feel quicker up and down the court, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down crawling around or being on my feet all day. So when they made it sound like a problem, I was kind of surprised.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what you currently weigh, and whether that’s changed over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know the exact number off the top of my head… the work physical is what made it a thing, really. The doctor there said I was too low, and I kind of remember thinking, “Low compared to what?” because I’m still doing my job, you know?\n\nLike at the garage, being under cars and on my feet all day, I feel lighter, not dragged down. Same with basketball — I feel quicker getting up and down the court. So yeah, I guess it has gone down some over the past few months, but I didn’t really see it as a problem until that physical.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much weight you’ve lost over the past few months, even roughly?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t have a clean number for you. That’s part of why this feels strange, because I wasn’t really tracking it like that.\n\nThe work physical is what got me sitting here, honestly. The doctor there said my weight was too low, and I guess I heard it, but in my head I’m thinking, well, I’m still moving around fine. At basketball I feel lighter on my feet, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down when I’m up and down all day.\n\nSo… roughly, yeah, it’s gone down, but I don’t know how much to say exactly.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the weight loss was something you were intentionally trying to achieve, even if it meant staying below what might be a normal weight for your height and build?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I was trying to be, like, under some number. I don’t really think of it that way.\n\nI guess I do have my own way of doing things now, though. Most mornings it’s just coffee, then later maybe a small sandwich or half of whatever’s around. The shop runs late a lot, and then the house is busy when I get home, and honestly it just feels easier sticking to that. Like if I keep it simple, I feel better, less… I don’t know, thrown off.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’m choosing it in that sense, but I wasn’t sitting there thinking about a “normal weight” chart or anything.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me approximately what your current weight is, and how that compares to what you think is a normal or healthy weight for your height and build?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure of the exact number right now. The work physical is what kind of kicked all this off — the doctor there said my weight was too low for me, I guess for my height and build.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to see it that way, because at basketball I just feel lighter on my feet, like I can move better. And at the garage, you know, I’m crawling around, standing, bending all day, and I don’t feel as weighed down. So compared to what they’re calling normal or healthy, yeah, I guess I’m under that… I just don’t always feel like it’s a bad thing.\n\nDoctor: Even though you said you weren’t focused on a specific number, do you think you’re currently under what most people would consider a normal or healthy weight for someone your height?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess if you put it that way, probably. The doctor at the work physical said my weight was too low, and that’s really what got me here.\n\nIt’s just hard for me to think of it like “unhealthy,” because when I’m playing basketball I feel quicker, lighter on my feet. And at the garage, being up and down all day, leaning over cars and moving around, I don’t feel as weighed down. So… yeah, most people might say I’m under where I should be, but I don’t really feel it the same way.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what your height is and whether the doctor at your work physical mentioned how your current weight compares to the minimum normal weight for someone your height?\n\nPatient: I’m not great with the exact numbers off the top of my head. I mean, I know my height, but I don’t really keep track of it like that, and the work physical is what sort of got this whole thing started.\n\nThe doctor there said my weight was too low for me, like for my height, yeah. I don’t remember him saying it like “minimum normal weight” exactly, or maybe he did and I just kind of tuned out after “too low.” I was thinking about getting back to the garage because we were slammed that week.\n\nBut yeah, he was basically saying I was under where I should be. I just… I don’t feel it the same way, you know? Playing basketball, I feel lighter on my feet, and at work I don’t feel as weighed down moving around cars all day.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me your exact height and what your current weight is, so I can better understand how it compares?\n\nPatient: I don’t know the exact weight right now… I know that’s what you’re asking, but I haven’t really been keeping a number in my head.\n\nThe work physical is what got me here, really. That doctor said my weight was too low for me, like for my height. I kind of heard that and then my mind went to work stuff — we had a bunch of cars backed up at the garage, and I was thinking about getting back there.\n\nBut yeah, he said it was low. I just have a hard time feeling worried about it, because on the basketball court I feel lighter, like I can move better, and at work I don’t feel as weighed down crawling around and bending over cars all day.\n\nDoctor: Can you please tell me your exact height and your most recent weight measurement from your work physical?\n\nPatient: I know, you’re asking for the exact numbers… I don’t really have them in my head. The work physical is what got me here, though—the doctor said my weight was too low for my height.\n\nI guess I didn’t hang onto the actual number because I was more thinking about getting back to the garage, and then later I was thinking how I don’t feel bad exactly. Like, when I’m playing basketball I feel lighter on my feet, and at work I don’t feel as weighed down moving around cars all day. But yeah, he said it was too low.\n\nDoctor: Can you please give me your exact height in feet and inches, and your most recent weight in pounds from the work physical?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I really don’t have the exact feet-and-inches and pounds number in my head from that physical. I know that’s what you need.\n\nWhat I remember is the doctor there saying my weight was too low for my height, and that’s what pushed all this along. I guess I didn’t lock onto the number because I was thinking about getting back to the garage, and honestly part of me was thinking, well, I feel lighter on my feet playing basketball and not as weighed down at work, so it didn’t hit me the way he probably meant it to.\n\nDoctor: Can you please tell me your height in feet and inches and the exact weight in pounds that the doctor recorded during your work physical?\n\nPatient: I really don’t have the exact number, doctor. I know I keep circling around it, but the work physical is what got me sent here—the doctor there said my weight was too low for my height.\n\nI guess I didn’t hang on to the pounds or the feet-and-inches part because I was thinking about getting back to the garage. And honestly, I don’t feel that bad about it. On the basketball court I feel lighter on my feet, and at work I don’t feel as weighed down crawling around cars all day. So I know he said it was too low, but the exact weight… I don’t know it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Anorexia Nervosa. You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it
